The Aether Golem Demonstration

Developing Crown plan to publicly demonstrate the Aether Golem's ability to consume a god, using the divine presence in Blackthorn as the target on 26 Pollant 301

The Aether Golem Demonstration

Quick Reference

Status: active

Mastermind:

Target: Blackthorn

Location: Blackthorn

Overview

What the plan is and who is behind it. The Crown built The Aether Golem, a two-part construct, to absorb a god and weaponize the harvested divine power. The golem body is already positioned at Blackthorn's central training grounds. The Aether Core is held at Crown Works Installation Seven. A public demonstration is planned for 26 Pollant 301 that will consume the god currently in Blackthorn.

Phases: 1. Construct and position the golem body. (Complete.) 2. Hold the Aether Core at Crown Works Installation Seven until the demonstration day. 3. Demonstration on 26 Pollant 301: assemble both components at the training grounds and consume the Blackthorn god, gaining weaponized divine power for the Crown.

The party can intervene by accessing or destroying the Aether Core at Crown Works Installation Seven, disrupting the demonstration site, or using Cassimir Blackthorn's knowledge of the god to act before 26 Pollant.

Player Characters

Calanthe Orchidaceae

Developing Human PC, adopted daughter of House Potentilla of Cinquefoil, whose Florescence was denied after five years of work

Calanthe Orchidaceae

Quick Reference

Player: Irais

Class: College of Dance Bard

Level: 5

Ancestry: Human

Background: Noble

Backstory Hooks: Florescence denied after five years with no public explanation, Aeneas Solanaceae's motives are hidden from her, A plateau in her potential was noticed by tutors and never explained, Not formally named heir to House Potentilla

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play. Calanthe Orchidaceae is a Human adopted as a young child by the heads of House Potentilla, the ruling house of Cinquefoil. Her surname is Orchidaceae rather than Potentilla because she has not been formally named heir. Her adoptive parents chose her from an orphanage, drawn by what they saw as exceptional potential. They love her and have not wavered in that. She grew up with every advantage the city's most powerful house could offer. Calanthe excelled through most of her years at The Academy, the school where children of prominent Elarosean families are educated in skills, arts, and the social expectations of their class. Her tutors eventually noticed a plateau in her improvement. She never broke through it. Her parents interpret this as a late bloom.

She struggled in her arts classes throughout. She passed them, not on merit. House Potentilla's standing made it easy for instructors to find reasons to give adequate marks, and nobody said so plainly.

In her final year she began her Florescence, the capstone work required of every Academy graduate. After five years, the work was denied. The years have piled up without explanation. People notice, but questions about it carry risk when the family behind her is as broad-reaching as it is, and most choose silence.

She found some comfort in Aeneas Solanaceae, a Human man from the fallen House Solanaceae. She does not know he selected her deliberately, drawn by her family's position and the prospect of what she might become.

  • Florescence denied after five years; no explanation has been given publicly, and House Potentilla's influence has kept the silence intact.
  • Aeneas Solanaceae is closer than she knows, and not for the reasons she believes.
  • Her tutors noticed a plateau in her potential that has never been explained.
  • She has not been named heir to House Potentilla, and the succession of Cinquefoil remains open.
  • Played by Irais. Calanthe is a level 6 Bard with the Noble background.

Cleke

Stub Party member who looted a longbow and emerald ring with IOUs, overheard Aeneas's blackmail, and tailed him with Dez to Sable.

Cleke

Quick Reference

Player: Chris

Class: Swarmkeeper Ranger

Level: 5

Ancestry: Aarakocra

Background: Wayfarer

Backstory Hooks: Memories of a previous life as a pilot, including the crash that ended it, Premonition of Great Change received from the Appori Enclave before his outward journey, Bond with Alice, honorary elder of the Appori Enclave, who found him in the peaks

Concept

Cleke is winged. His wings gave out when he attempted to scale the east garden wall of House Potentilla on 1 Caducan 301 Bloom, and the noise drew a guard. No other physical description appears in current records. An archer who cannot see what he shoots, as Magister Ilex Verbena put it when she named the party from her ledger-book in the orange orchard. He is also the party's eye for the arcane. At Potentilla Manor he cast Detect Magic and picked out the identical faint abjuration aura behind every city-green guard's lapel, which none of the house staff carried. He and Dez walked past the guards to test them, and he listened at the grill off the linen passage with the rest. He is new enough to Cinquefoil's past that Calanthe Orchidaceae had to explain The Grey Wilt to him. When the party weighed its options in Vanda's study, he proposed sneaking into the workroom.

Goals & Bonds

What they want, and who or what they are tied to.

Backstory

Where they come from, in brief. Cleke carries memories from a previous life in which he was a pilot. That life ended in a crash. He awoke in the Apporiclave Peaks, southwest of Elarosea beneath the Imperial Shelf, and was found by Alice, an honorary elder of the Appori Enclave. After time among the Appori, he was invited to undertake the enclave's traditional outward journey. Before setting out, he received a premonition themed around "Great Change". He donned his ceremonial blindfold and walked the road, arriving in Benz on 7 Pollant 301 Bloom, where he met the rest of the party.

Dez

Developing Cynical former Roots-dweller from Loquat, son of a Lake People spy and an Elarosean soldier father who vanished leading Thorns away

Dez

Quick Reference

Player: Angelo

Class: Draconic Sorceror

Level: 5

Ancestry: human

Background: Raised in the Roots of Loquat; left the city as hired help on a trading company

Backstory Hooks: Father's fate unknown after diverting the Queen's Thorns, Mother Alicia organizes the Rooted in Loquat under the Scribes' Union, Deep contempt for the Crown, the nobility, and anyone who wields authority over the poor

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play. Dez (given name Dexter) is a human born in the Roots of Loquat, the squalid lower district beneath the privileged Canopy. His mother Alicia was a spy originally dispatched from the Lake People into Elarosea. His father was an unnamed Elarosean soldier who led pursuing Queen's Thorns away from the family when Dez was young, and was never seen again. Growing up in the Roots gave Dez firsthand exposure to how the "Cannies" (the wealthy scholastic class above) treat the "Rooted" below. He is cynical by nature and holds deep contempt for authority, the upper class, and the Crown. Alicia saved enough to send Dez out of Loquat. He took work as hired help with a small trading company and eventually arrived at Benz in time for the Festival of Shooting Stars. The crash and massacre pulled him into events far beyond a trading run, and he is now a fugitive alongside the rest of the party.

  • His father was last seen drawing Queen's Thorns away from the family. His fate is unknown.
  • Alicia remains in Loquat and has been quietly working to organize the Rooted under the Scribes' Union since Dez left. Her activities could attract Thorn attention.
  • Why were the Thorns following Dez's family to begin with?

Kirill Amaterasu

Developing Young human monk from the Verdant Mendicants, arrived in Benz on his first solo mission to help a farmer's cow give birth

Kirill Amaterasu

Quick Reference

Player: Tai

Class: Way of the Sun Soul Monk

Level: 5

Ancestry: human

Background: Member of the Verdant Mendicants, nomads living in the southern forests outside Elarosea

Backstory Hooks: First solo mission, swept into far larger events, Connection to the Verdant Mendicants and their way of life outside Elarosea

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play. Kirill Amaterasu is a young human monk raised among the Verdant Mendicants, a roaming community that keeps to the forested lands south of Elarosea's border. He arrived in Benz on his first solo assignment: help a local farmer's cow deliver a calf. The crash during the Festival of Shooting Stars and the subsequent massacre by the Queen's Thorns pulled him into something far beyond that errand. His original task was simple and practical. He is now a wanted fugitive far from his community and facing a political situation his upbringing among the nomadic Verdant Mendicants gave him little preparation for. His bond to the Mendicants and their values outside Elarosean society may shape how he navigates the Crown, the city-states, and the party.

Layla

Developing Vice Captain of the Caelrosa guard, secretly an office worker from another world who died of overwork and woke in Layla's body

Layla

Quick Reference

Player: CJ

Class: Oath of Devotion Paladin

Level: 5

Ancestry: human

Background: Vice Captain of the guard, Caelrosa; carries full memories of a former life in another world

Backstory Hooks: Remembers her past life in full, except the moment of her death, Inhabiting a body with relationships and a rank she did not personally earn, Knowledge from another world may surface as insight or confusion

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play. Layla holds the rank of Vice Captain of the guard in Caelrosa. In truth, her consciousness belongs to an office worker from another world who died of overwork and awoke in this body with her prior life's memories intact, save the manner of her death. She arrived in Benz on an official posting to supplement short-staffed local law enforcement. Before waking in Layla's body, she was an office worker in what appears to be a wholly separate world. She died of overwork. She retains all memories of that life except the moment of death itself, a gap that may matter later. In Caelrosa she had risen to Vice Captain of the guard, though those experiences belong to a person she was not.

  • She does not know how she died or why she woke in Layla's body.
  • The original Layla had a rank, relationships, and a history in Caelrosa that the player character has not lived through.
  • Her previous world's knowledge has no obvious place in Elarosea and may surface unexpectedly.

Val

Developing Human PC from Blackthorn, former banner knight with a violence-first moral code; joined the party formally in Loquat following the defeat of the beast on 17 Pollant 301

Val

Quick Reference

Player: Jake

Class: Path of the Zealot Barbarian

Level: 5

Ancestry: Human

Background: Banner knight

Backstory Hooks: His sister back in Blackthorn, who he believed deserved better than what the world gave her, The debt he feels toward Vesperae for the first unsolicited kindness he had known outside his family

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play.

Goals & Bonds

What they want, and who or what they are tied to.

Backstory

Where they come from, in brief. Val grew up in the working districts pressed against Blackthorn's walls, where his father tanned hides, his mother wove cloth, and his sister drew the short end of what life offered. They were the whole of his warmth. Outside that circle, the world taught him that violence is ordinary and harm is just how things get done. He left Blackthorn as a banner knight, seeking fights that would make him sharper. He found one in a deep forest, lost badly, and was left bleeding out. A stranger found him, nursed him back to health, and asked for nothing. It was the first kindness he had ever received from someone who was not family. That stranger was Vesperae. Val's code has one hard edge: family and innocents are off limits. Everyone else is fair game if the situation calls for it. He does not think of this as cruelty; it is just how the world works. His bond to Vesperae is the only friendship he has formed outside his blood, and it is the reason he is with the party at all.

  • 17 Pollant 301 Bloom: Arrived as relief assistance following the fall of Loquat and joined the party alongside Vesperae.

Vesperae Locke

Established Aasimar daughter of Magister Corbin Locke and member of House Locke of Loquat; joined the party formally in Loquat after the beast's defeat; father was assassinated in his coma on 19 Pollant 301

Vesperae Locke

Quick Reference

Player: Julie

Class: Twilight Domain Cleric

Level: 5

Ancestry: Aasimar

Background: Canopy noble turned outside-system reformer

Backstory Hooks: father's assassination and unknown perpetrator, Corbin's covert resistance network and its surviving members, the Locke family's exploitative legacy in Loquat, Corbin's undisclosed compromises and acceptable losses, her father's assassination and its unknown author, her debt of kindness to Val and his to her

Concept

One line on who this hero is and how they play. Vesperae grew up among the spires and hanging gardens of The Canopy, the elevated district of Loquat where wealthy families live suspended above the desert. Her family, House Locke, were architects of that district's social order for generations. She is the daughter of Magister Corbin Locke, a man who broke from the family pattern and tried to dismantle from within what the Lockes had built. She left Loquat approximately 15 Solvance 300 Bloom, deciding that her father would work within the system and she would work outside it, catching those it dropped. Her driving goal is to continue Magister Corbin Locke's work. She has sworn an oath to carry his legacy: protecting those the system discards and dismantling the conditions that made his death possible. She travels with the party to pursue that oath and to find and take revenge on those behind her father's assassination. The Locke family built and enforced The Canopy's social supremacy for generations, profiting from The Roots labor and framing the arrangement as natural order. Her grandfather was the family's most notorious figure, a man who observed starving children in the Roots and described the situation as an inefficient allocation of resources.

Her father, Magister Corbin Locke, was different. He began quietly redirecting resources, protecting individuals, and building a network of small resistances within the institutions he occupied. Vesperae grew up watching him try to undo what his name had done, though she does not know the full scope of his compromises or what he considered acceptable loss.

She left Loquat around 15 Solvance 300 Bloom, deciding she would be more useful outside the system her father navigated. Where he worked within it, she would catch those it dropped.

  • Who are the surviving members of Corbin's quiet resistance network, and do they know he is dead?
  • What compromises did Corbin accept to maintain his position, and what did those choices cost others?
  • How does House Locke's reputation shape how Vesperae is received, in a devastated Loquat and beyond?
  • Who ordered the assassination, and why was the timing tied to the beast attack?
  • Pollant 301 Bloom: Returned to Loquat to find it devastated. Helped survivors. Magister Corbin Locke expended himself to help the party defeat the beast that had attacked the city and fell into a coma. He was assassinated before she could reach him. Swore an oath to continue his legacy and joined the party. (See Beast Attack on Loquat, Assassination of Corbin Locke.)

Reference

The Drowned Oath

Developing A self-contained folk-horror oneshot for three or four level-3 players, set in the Luneglass Fjord among the Lake People, in which an old bargain with the thing beneath the ice comes due in a single night.

The Drowned Oath

Quick Reference

Kind:

---

How to use this doc

Everything you need to run tonight lives in this one file. Nothing else in the vault is required. Read it top to bottom once, then run from the act sections; the boxed text is meant to be read aloud, the rest is for your eyes.

The adventure runs on one night, in three acts:

1. The Ice-Wake (Act 1) establishes the community's warmth and the beauty of the rite, sincerely, before any horror. Its job is to make you and the players love these people so the ending costs something. 2. The Cracks (Act 2) is investigation. The players learn what is really happening under the ice and uncover the lie at the heart of it. 3. The Last Water (Act 3) is the rite itself, the three-way choice, and the set-piece climax.

A visible clock, the Sealing, ticks the whole time. When it fills, the last open water freezes and the choice is made for them, badly.

Optional campaign hooks (leave these out to run it cold)

This is written to stand alone, but it slots into the main campaign if you want it to. The Luneglass Fjord is canon; Alicia was a Lake People operative sent into Elarosea, and her son Dez plays in the main game. Iskra (pregen 3) is written in the same intelligence tradition Alicia came from. You can run this as Dez's mother's home a generation before she left, or as a flashback, or ignore all of it.

---

Content warnings and safety

This is folk-horror. The engine of the story is a community that sacrifices one of its own and tells itself a loving story about it. Handle it with the table's consent, not around it.

Before Act 1, take two minutes:

  • Name the themes plainly: ritual human sacrifice, drowning, bodies kept whole in ice, a beloved mentor's betrayal, cold and the threat of starvation.
  • Ask for lines (never on screen) and veils (happens, but we cut away). Common ones here: the moment of a drowning shown in detail, harm to the youngest characters, the Given's fear if you would rather keep them serene.
  • Put an X-card, a pause phrase, or an open "stop and check in" rule on the table and mean it.
  • Say out loud that there is no correct ending. The three-way choice at the climax is a tone question, not a morality test you are secretly grading. Do not sneak in a right answer.

The worst thing that happens in a fully failed session is that the fjord faces a hard, honest winter and grief. Keep the dread in the fiction; keep the table safe.

---

The pitch (what the players know)

It is the deepest week of winter in the Luneglass Fjord, the season the Lake People call the Long Dark. The meltwater lakes lie under a hand's-width of black ice you can see straight down through. Tonight is the Stilling, the rite the fjord holds once a generation, when the community walks one of its own out onto the ice and gives them to the water. In return the deep keeps a warm current running under the freeze, and the fish keep coming, and no one starves through the Long Dark.

It is not murder, to the Lake People. It is the oldest love they have. The chosen one is the Given, and to be Given is the highest honor the fjord can pay a person. The lot was drawn a month ago. It fell on a young weaver named Wren Underlake, who has spent the month being feasted, painted, and prepared, and who is, by every account, at peace.

The player characters are of the fjord, born to it. Tonight they gather for the Ice-Wake, the long feast that ends when the Given is walked out. Except the ice has started to sing at night. And this morning, an old Given surfaced inside the glass, whole and open-eyed, a generation after the water took them.

The GM truth (the knife)

The pact is real, and older than the fjord's memory of making it. Something vast and patient lives in the deep water under the glacier. The Lake People call her She Beneath the Glass, or the Stillmother, or just the deep. For as long as anyone can count, she has kept the current warm through winter in exchange for one soul a generation, walked out and given to the Mouth, the single hole in the ice that never freezes.

A generation ago, the Keeper cheated. The lot that cycle fell on a child. The Keeper, Ondre Vael, could not do it. She smuggled the child out and drowned a dying stranger in the Given's place, spoke the rite over the wrong soul, and told no one. For thirty years she has carried it alone, and the fjord has prospered on a debt it does not know it owes.

The deep has been counting. A given soul is a given soul; a substitute is a theft. This winter she is calling the original debt with interest. The current under the ice is dying. The lake is closing into one solid sheet for the first time in living memory, and the old Given are rising back up through the glass, whole, as her notice: the account is short, and she is coming to collect the whole fjord if she is not paid tonight.

The spared child is still alive. She grew up. She is Maren, the fjord's ice-reader, beloved, forty years old, and she has no idea she was ever the Given. The debt the deep actually wants is Maren, the soul that was owed and never paid, not Wren, whom the lot chose this cycle. That is the horror the players will uncover, and the knot the climax turns on.

Over one night the players learn the truth, decide who they will protect, and at the Mouth they choose:

  • Pay the lot. Give Wren, as the rite demands this cycle. The deep may or may not accept a fresh soul against an old debt.
  • Pay the debt. Give Maren, the soul that was truly owed. Restores the original bargain. Costs the fjord its kindest woman and asks the players to finish what Ondre could not.
  • Cheat. Trick the deep again, as Ondre did. She is wise to it now. High risk, and if it fails she takes more.
  • Break the oath. Sever the pact by force. The fjord goes free, and the current dies, and next winter is honest, cold, and hungry. Freedom with a body count you will not see tonight.

There is no clean answer. Build all four from love.

---

The Sealing (the ticking clock)

Draw six boxes where the players can see them, or track them privately if you prefer the dread of not knowing. This is the lake freezing shut. When the sixth box fills, the Mouth ices over and the rite becomes impossible; jump to the ending The Long Dark Comes In.

Advance the Sealing by one box when any of these happens:

  • The party finishes Act 1 (one box banked automatically as the night deepens).
  • A scene ends on a hard investigative dead end or a wasted stretch of time.
  • The party fails a group check or triggers a hazard badly in Act 2.
  • Each full round of the climax rite in Act 3 that does not resolve it.

Advance it by two if the party spends a long time in open combat during Act 2 while the ice groans around them.

Read the pressure as it climbs. One line of weather each time, no more:

  • Box 2: "The wind drops. The lake goes quiet the way a held breath is quiet."
  • Box 4: "New ice creeps across the last black water at the Mouth, a finger's width every hour."
  • Box 5: "You can hear it now under everything, a low grinding, the whole lake closing like a jaw."
  • Box 6: the Mouth seals. See the ending.

---

The fjord (location key)

The community is small, maybe two hundred souls, wintering close together on the shore and out onto the ice itself. Five places matter tonight.

The Longhall (the Wake-House)

A single low building of driftwood and whale-rib and sod, dug half into the shore, with a peat fire that never goes out. Warm, crowded, smells of smoked fish and pine tar and wet wool. This is where the Ice-Wake happens. Everyone you love in this story is in this room at the start.

The Given-House

A small hut of packed snow and ice-block, out on the near ice, lit blue from within by a single lamp. Wren has lived here alone for the month of preparation, fasting, being painted with the old marks. It is quiet, reverent, and cold. Wren is calm here. Too calm, if the players look closely (Insight): she has been given something in her food to keep her serene.

The Glass

The frozen lake itself. A hand's-width of black ice you can see through to green-black water. This is the board most of the horror plays out on. The old Given are surfacing here, drifting up under the ice with their eyes open, whole and unrotted. Walking the Glass is a hazard (see Black Ice and Killing Cold). At night the ice sings, a high pure ringing, as the deep pushes up against it.

The Undercroft (the Keeper's cave)

A shrine cut into the glacier's foot, all blue ice and old carvings, where Ondre Vael keeps the rite. Behind a false wall of ice-block she keeps her records: thirty years of a Keeper's private ledger, and the one page she should have burned. This is the heart of the Act 2 investigation.

The Mouth (the Last Water)

Far out on the ice, the one hole that never freezes, a black eye of open water three strides across, ringed with old standing stones worn to nubs. This is where the Stilling happens. This is where the deep comes closest to the surface. This is the climax.

---

Cast

Each NPC wants one clear thing. Play the want.

Ondre Vael, the Keeper (she/her)

Old, spare, tired in a way that goes past the body. Priest of the deep, keeper of the rite, and the woman who cheated a generation ago out of love and has never once slept easy since. Wants: the debt paid quietly tonight, without anyone learning what she did. She will steer the party away from the Undercroft, will lie by omission, and will crack if confronted with the ledger. She is not a villain. She is what love does when it is asked to do the impossible.

  • Voice: speaks in the certainties of liturgy, then falters when the certainties are tested. "The deep does not bargain twice. It only remembers." A confession, if you get one, is quiet and dry-eyed: "I traded a stranger for a child and called it mercy. I have been afraid of the ice for thirty years."

Wren Underlake, the Given (they/them)

Young, luminous, serene. Wren has made peace, or has been helped to. Wants: to be worthy of the honor, and to spare their sister Néve the grief. Wren is the emotional center. If a player reaches them honestly (an emotional beat), the serenity can crack, and under it is a frightened young person who does not want to die and has never once said so out loud. That crack is the whole session, so hold it for a real moment.

  • Voice: gentle, rehearsed, then suddenly not. "I have known since the lot fell. I am not afraid." Later, if reached: "Don't tell Néve I said this."

Maren, the ice-reader (she/her)

Forty, weathered, beloved, funny. Reads the lake's moods better than anyone alive, which is a bitter irony given what she is. Wants: to help the fjord through a bad night; she has no idea she is the debt. When the players uncover the truth, Maren is the person it lands on hardest, and how she takes it is the soul of the pay-the-debt ending. Play her as someone who has lived thirty borrowed years without knowing they were borrowed, and now must decide what to do with the bill.

  • Voice: dry, warm, quick. "The ice sings when the deep can't sleep. Tonight it's screaming, so." Later: "A whole life. A good one. And it was never mine to keep."

Old Sesk, the elder (he/him)

Ancient, hard, the only person in the fjord who says out loud that the Stilling is murder. Wants: the pact broken, tonight, whatever it starves. He is the voice of the break ending, and he is not wrong, and he is not kind about it. Give the players a real argument here, not a strawman.

  • Voice: flat, unsentimental. "We drown our own and call it weather. Break the thing. Let the winter be honest for once."

Tolv and Bec, the net-menders (the bench)

Two old men who have been married to their work and to arguing with each other for fifty years. They mend nets in the corner of the Longhall and bet on absolutely everything, in shells. Their only job is to react. Use them to keep any slow scene alive, to place absurd bets on the players during Act 1, and, at the climax, to go silent. When Tolv and Bec stop betting, the table knows the tone has turned, and you never had to say so.

---

Act 1: The Ice-Wake

Goal: make the players love this community and this Given before you threaten either. Bond them to at least one NPC each. End on the first crack of wrongness.

Open in the warm. Read aloud:

The Longhall

Cold, frigid air blows through the gaps of a sealskin flap; the entrance to the Long Hall, a single low building of driftwood and whale-rib and sod, dug half into the shore, with a peat fire that never goes out. It is warm, crowded, smells of smoked fish and pine tar and wet wool. The whole fjord is in this one long room, two hundred people shoulder to shoulder, and every one of them is talking and breathing life into the room. There is a girl by the fire with her face painted in the old blue marks, and she is laughing at something, and she is the most alive person you have ever seen. Néve, that is your sister. Tomorrow she belongs to the lake, and tonight she is laughing, and the room has decided, together, without a word, that tonight that is allowed to be enough.

Where each of them is (opening vignettes)

After the establishing box, before the feast gets moving, place each character in the room. Read each player their own vignette, or hand it over. These are starting positions and a first beat, not a script; the moment each one ends on is the player's to take. Each opens toward that character's spotlight beat in the set-piece below. At a three-person table, use the three you are running.

The Sibling.

Néve, at the fire

You have not let Wren out of your sight all night, and you are pretending that you have not. Here they are, right where you can watch them, at the fire, the blue marks bright on their face, laughing with the little ones who keep daring each other to touch the paint. Someone put a cup in your hand an hour ago. It is still full. Wren catches you looking, the way they always catch you looking, and crosses the room and leans their shoulder into yours the way they have done since they were small enough to reach up to do it. "You're doing the face," they say. "The Néve face. Don't." The room is warm and singing and glad, and your sibling smells of paint and smoke, and they are alive against your side, and tomorrow the fjord walks them onto the ice. Néve, they asked you not to make a scene. They did not ask you to let go.

The Apprentice.

Corvann, at the Keeper's table

The Keeper's things are laid out on the long table at the back, where the heat does not quite reach: the blue paint-pots, the cord, the old bone comb, the words you will help speak tomorrow. You have been checking them and rechecking them, which is your task tonight, and which is also something to do with your hands. Ondre Vael stands over them beside you, small and straight-backed, and she has not eaten, and she has not sung, and when the room laughs she does not seem to hear it. You have known her your whole life. You have never once seen her afraid. "Corvann," she says, without looking up from the paint, and then she stops, as if she has forgotten what she meant to ask, or thought better of it. Her hand is resting on the cord. It is shaking, very slightly, and she is trying to hold it still. Corvann, tomorrow is supposed to be the proudest day of your life.

The Outsider.

Iskra, along the cold wall

You came back three days ago and the fjord still does not quite know where to put you, so you have found the place you always find: the bench along the cold wall where the singers sit, half in the light and half out of it, close enough to belong and far enough to watch. The grief-songs are going now, the old ones, and here is the strange thing you had forgotten and are remembering. You know them. Not from here. You know them from under. Every time the room lands on a low note a second voice lands with it, lower, further down, patient, and only you can hear it, and it is glad you are home. It has been glad for three days. It calls you by the name it has always called you. Iskra, listener, the room is warm and the people are singing, and the thing beneath the ice is singing too, and you are the only soul at this wake who hears both.

The Skeptic.

Sable, at the fire with a full kill

You brought the last of the hunt in over your shoulder an hour ago, seal-fat and dark meat for a feast you do not believe in, and you laid it at the fire anyway, because Wren is going to eat well on their last night if you have anything to say about it. Your grandfather found you the moment you came through the flap. Old Sesk does not say hello. He says it low, so only you can hear it under the singing: "Look at them. All of them. Singing that child toward the water and calling it a gift." He is looking at Wren. His hand is tight on your arm. You know the argument. You have had it a hundred times, and you have always been on his side, and neither of you has ever done one thing about it. Sable, across the fire your great-uncle Halber went onto this same ice before you were born, and tonight every face in this room is smiling.

Let the players move through the feast. This is the set-piece.

Set-piece: warming the Wake (mood meter)

The obstacle (in fiction): carry the wake. Give this doomed community one good last night, and in doing so earn a bond that will matter at the Mouth.

Engine: mood meter. Track a five-step dial, the Warmth of the Wake: Hollow, Strained, Holding, Bright, Blazing. Start at Holding (3).

This is low-stakes and you should say so with your body language, not your words. The players are not fighting anything. They are making a wake worth remembering. The scoreboard is a pretext; the prize is the moment.

Run three beats, or four at a full table (keep each quick so the meter still swings). Each player takes one beat as their spotlight, though anyone can buy in:

  • The Feast (spotlight: the Given's sibling): a toast, a shared memory, a dance. CHA (Performance or Persuasion) or WIS (a true thing said plainly).
  • The Given's table (spotlight: the cleric): sit with Wren, or with the elders arguing over them. CHA or WIS (Insight to really see Wren) or INT (Religion, to speak the rite's beauty back to the room).
  • The old songs (spotlight: the warlock): the fjord's grief-songs, which Iskra half-remembers from the outside and half-hears from somewhere deeper. CHA (Performance) or a real-skill path: a player who actually sings, hums, or speaks a genuine verse takes the beat outright, no roll.
  • The hunter's gift (spotlight: the skeptic, at a four-player table): Sable lays out the last meat of the hunt at the fire and has to decide whether to let the family grievance rest for one night, for Wren's sake. STR or DEX (Survival or Athletics, the provision laid out well) or WIS or CHA (a hard toast, the grudge set down in front of everyone who knows he carries it).

Per-round dial movement (by result tier, not pass/fail):

  • 7 or below: down one. "The room feels the grief poke through. Someone looks at the door."
  • 8 to 12: hold. A fine moment, no swing.
  • 13 to 16: up one. The room leans in.
  • 17 to 20: up one and advantage on the next beat. Tolv slaps the table and doubles his bet.
  • 21+: up two. Even Old Sesk almost smiles.

Free +1 beats (GM discretion, once each): a callback to something another player established, a real bit of voice or song, an honest emotional turn instead of a joke, naming a specific fjord detail (a person, a boat, a dish) and making it matter.

Cooperation hook ("set the table"): a player not in the spotlight can buy into the active roll with real added fiction (a harmony, a second on the toast, tending Wren's cup). Any valid help grants advantage; a genuinely good one also nudges the dial.

What the dial buys (this is the point): each player who lands a real moment with a specific NPC forms a bond. Write down who bonded with whom. A bond is worth one advantage die at the climax on a roll made to protect, reach, or speak for that NPC. Blazing at the end of the wake means the whole party also starts Act 3 with one shared reroll, "the wake remembers." A Hollow wake costs nothing mechanical, but play the fjord colder for it.

Stakes: pride and a warm memory. Nothing dies here. Do not sneak in a threat.

The first crack

End Act 1 on the knife. When the wake has peaked, or when a player is mid-laugh, cut to it. Read aloud:

Under the ice

The song is still going when the child screams. Not a hurt scream, a pointing scream, the kind that turns two hundred heads at once toward the same patch of floor. The Longhall is built out over the near ice, and under the skin rugs, under the black glass you have danced on your whole life, a face is looking up at you. A man. Whole. Unrotted. His eyes are open and his mouth is open and his hands are pressed flat to the underside of the ice as if he has been trying the whole time to get back in. Old Sesk says the name before anyone else can. "That's Halber. That's my brother Halber. We gave him to the lake forty years ago." The fire is very loud. Nobody is singing now.

Bank the first box of the Sealing. Move to Act 2.

---

Act 2: The Cracks

Goal: the players uncover, in roughly this order: the pact is real and dying; the lake is sealing shut; the Keeper cheated a generation ago; the spared child lived and is Maren; the deep wants the true debt tonight. They do not need every piece to reach the climax, but the more they have, the more the choice at the Mouth means.

This is open and non-linear. The players will follow leads across the fjord. Reward any reasonable approach; each thread is keyed to a different skill so every pregen shines somewhere. Use fail-forward: a missed check costs time (advance the Sealing) or comfort (a hazard), never a hard stop.

Thread: the surfacing Given (the Glass)

Going out to examine the bodies in the ice. WIS (Medicine) or INT (Investigation): the Given are whole because the deep preserved them, and they are all rising now, all at once, which means the deep is emptying its account. A DC 13 read notices the newest body is not old at all: it is the "stranger" Ondre drowned, dressed in Given's marks, and the marks are wrong, painted by someone in a hurry. First thread to the cheat. Hazard: the Glass is Black Ice; the deep's hands may reach for anyone who lingers (see hazards).

Thread: the Given-House (Wren)

Sitting with Wren. WIS (Insight): Wren is drugged calm, and under it, terrified. CHA (a real conversation): Wren lets slip that Ondre visited last night and wept, and would not say why, and kept starting to say "I did this before" and stopping. Emotional-beat territory; if a player reaches Wren honestly, let the serenity break for real and hold the table there. Wren does not know the truth, but knows Ondre is hiding one.

Thread: the Undercroft (the ledger)

The heart of it. Ondre will steer them off; getting in past her means CHA (persuade or deceive her), DEX (Stealth) while she is at the wake, or simply asking Maren, who has a key and no idea what it guards. Inside, behind a false ice wall (INT Investigation or WIS Perception DC 14, or just searching thoroughly): the Keeper's ledger. Thirty years of entries, and one page that reads like a wound. The handout (see below) is this page. It records, in Ondre's own hand, that the lot fell on a child named for the ice-reader's craft, that she could not do it, that she gave the deep a dying wayfarer instead, and that the child lived. It does not name Maren outright. It names the year. Anyone who does the arithmetic (INT, or just counting on fingers) lands on Maren's age.

Thread: the ice-reader (Maren)

Maren is the party's best source on the lake itself: the current has stopped, she says, for the first time in her life, and the ice is sealing from the shores inward toward the Mouth. She can date the change to this winter. She does not know she is the answer to her own mystery. The gut-punch is scripted: whichever thread reveals the spared child's identity, it is Maren, and ideally a player is the one who realizes it while standing next to her. Do not rush past it.

Thread: Old Sesk (the argument)

Sesk has always believed the Stilling is murder, and now his dead brother is looking up through the floor. He wants the pact broken tonight. He is the break ending's advocate and its conscience: he knows breaking it means a hungry winter, and he says so, and he wants it anyway. "Better we starve honest than eat what the lake sells us."

Confronting Ondre

However the players assemble it, aim the act at a confrontation with Ondre Vael. Confronted with the ledger or the arithmetic, she breaks quietly. She tells them the rest: the deep does not accept a substitute against a true debt. It wants Maren, the soul it was promised and never given. Wren's lot this cycle is real, but the deep is not calling this cycle. It is calling the old one. Ondre believes there are only two honest options: give Maren, or give up the fjord. She cannot bring herself to say the third out loud, that Wren dies for nothing if the deep only wants Maren. Let the players notice that themselves.

By the end of Act 2 the players should be holding an unbearable, well-understood choice, and the Sealing should be at box 4 or 5. Push them to the Mouth.

---

Act 3: The Last Water

Goal: the choice, and the rite that enacts it. Everyone the players bonded with is here. The lake is a jaw closing. This is a tone question, not a test. Run all four endings from love.

Read aloud as they reach the Mouth:

The Mouth

Out past the last standing stone the ice stops, and the water begins, a black eye three strides across that has never once frozen, ringed by stones worn down to teeth. Everything else has gone still. No wind. No song. The whole lake has closed behind you into one white sheet, and only this last dark circle is left open, and it is smaller than it was an hour ago. Néve, you can see the new ice growing across it from the edges, a finger's width while you watch. Down in the black, something pale is rising, slow, patient, the size of the idea of a face. It does not break the surface. It does not have to. You can feel it reading the room, counting the people on the ice, deciding whether tonight it will be paid or fed.

Then let them choose. Do not call for a decision faster than the table wants to give one. Let Wren, Maren, Ondre, and Sesk each say their piece.

The rite (shared group challenge)

Whatever the players choose, enacting it at the Mouth uses the same engine, reskinned by the choice. This keeps the climax mechanically tense without an HP race a drowned god would win against three or four level-3 characters.

Engine: shared accumulating-success. The party needs 4 successes before 3 failures. Each round, each player makes one check using a skill they can justify in the fiction, and a different skill is required each round, so no one leans on the same tool twice. Voice every success and failure as a beat, never a bare number.

What the checks are keyed to changes with the choice:

  • Pay (Wren or Maren): the checks are the rite itself, spoken and held while the deep tests the givers' resolve and the ice tries to take more than it is owed. WIS and CHA (holding the words, holding the Given's hand, holding the line), STR (keeping footing as the ice bucks), INT/Religion (getting the liturgy right). The Given walks out willingly; the challenge is the party enacting a loving death without flinching or losing anyone else to the water.
  • Cheat: an open-floor challenge first. The players must actually propose a trick the table believes: a substitute, an illusion, a false soul, a bargain reworded. A genuinely clever plan the GM buys lowers the successes needed to 3 and grants advantage; a thin one raises the failure risk. The deep is wise to substitution now, so a straight repeat of Ondre's trick auto-fails the first round. Iskra's link to the patron is the wedge: they can speak her tongue, which is the only reason a cheat has any chance at all.
  • Break: a tug-of-war of wills against She Beneath the Glass, layered over the group challenge. Five-box track, marker at center. Each round the party's lead (usually Iskra, turning on their own patron) rolls WIS (Insight) or CHA (Intimidation) against the deep, while the others hold off the drowned Given rising through the ice (combat, staggered, one Given per failed round; see hazards). Push the marker off the deep's end and the pact snaps.

The bonds pay off here. Every bond formed at the wake is one advantage die on a roll made to protect, reach, or speak for that NPC. A Blazing wake grants the shared "the wake remembers" reroll. This is why Act 1 was not a warm-up.

The Sealing keeps ticking. Each full round that does not resolve the rite advances it one box. If it hits six mid-climax, the Mouth seals under their hands; see The Long Dark Comes In.

Failure moves the scene, it never ends it. A failed round is a drowned Given hauling itself up through the crack, a giver slipping toward the water, the deep pressing a cold thought into someone's skull, the ice screaming. Reserve a true loss (a character actually pulled under) for a full 3-failure collapse, and even then let a companion spend everything to trade themselves for the drowning one if they choose, because that is exactly the kind of moment this whole night was built to produce.

Endings

Write the aftermath from whichever choice the party enacts. None is clean. None is graded.

Pay the lot (give Wren). The rite completes. Wren walks into the black and does not come up, and for one held breath nothing happens, and then the current turns. The lake warms. The ice at the Mouth stops growing. The fjord is saved this winter. But if the party learned the truth, they know the deep took a fresh soul against an old debt, and a debt paid with the wrong coin is a debt deferred, not cleared. The current runs warm for a while. Maren lives. Somewhere under the ice, the account is still short, and the players are the only ones who know the bill will come again. End on Néve.

Pay the debt (give Maren). The hardest ending and the truest to the bargain. Maren, told what she is, decides for herself; do not let the players force her, let her choose, because her choosing is the whole point. She walks out where a child should have walked forty years ago. The deep accepts her, the true soul, and the debt clears clean. The current returns strong. Wren lives, and will carry knowing they were spared and someone else paid. Ondre's thirty-year sin is finished, and it destroys her to watch it finish. The fjord is saved honestly, and it cost them the woman who read the ice. End on Maren's last dry joke, or on the silence after it.

Cheat. If the trick lands, the deep is fooled a second time and the party has bought the fjord another generation on borrowed credit, and they are now the ones carrying Ondre's secret, and the ledger has a new page in their hand. If it fails, the deep takes the substitute and reaches past it: read the failure as the water taking more than one, the ice closing on the givers, a cost the party chose by gambling. Cheating well is a triumph laced with dread. Cheating badly is a tragedy they authored.

Break the oath. The pact snaps. The pale thing sinks, and the singing stops, and the drowned Given go still in the glass at last, and it is over. No one is given tonight. The fjord is free. And Maren, who reads the lake, is the one who says it, quiet, into the new silence: the current is gone. Next winter the lake will freeze solid to the bottom, and the fish will not come, and the Long Dark will be honest and cold and hungry for the first time in three hundred years. Old Sesk got what he wanted and does not look glad. Freedom is real. So is the winter. End before you show the winter; let the table sit in what they chose.

The Long Dark comes in (the Sealing fills). If the clock beats them, the Mouth freezes over with the choice still in their hands. The deep, unpaid and uncheated and unbroken, simply takes what it is owed the slow way. Over the Long Dark the current dies, the fish vanish, and the fjord goes into a winter it will not all come out of. Play the last scene small: the party, the morning after, standing on a lake gone entirely white, no black water left anywhere, and the whole community behind them not yet knowing. This is the grim ending, and it is earned by inaction, not railroaded. Do not gloat. Let it be quiet.

---

Monsters and hazards

Tuned for a small party of three or four level-3 characters. A three-person party has a fragile action economy, so this leans on hazards and staggered threats rather than swarms; a fourth character eases that, and the notes below say where to add pressure. XP budget: for three, Easy 225, Medium 450, Hard 675, Deadly 1200; for four, Easy 300, Medium 600, Hard 900, Deadly 1600 (adjusted XP).

Drowned Given (custom, CR 1, 200 XP)

A soul the lake took and kept, risen through the ice. Whole, water-logged, patient, wrong.

Drowned Given

AC 12 | HP 33 (6d8+6) | Speed 20 ft, swim 40 ft

STR 14 (+2) DEX 10 (+0) CON 13 (+1) INT 6 (-2) WIS 10 (+0) CHA 6 (-2)

Damage resistances cold, bludgeoning from nonmagical weapons | Vulnerabilities fire | Condition immunities charmed, exhaustion, frightened, poisoned

Senses darkvision 60 ft, passive Perception 10 | Languages understands the fjord tongue, does not speak

Ice-Locked. Near a crack or the Mouth, the Given can sink into the water as a bonus action and resurface from any opening within 30 ft.

Actions

Drowning Grasp. Melee, +4 to hit, reach 5 ft. Hit: 7 (1d8+2) bludgeoning, and a Large or smaller target must make a DC 12 Strength save or be dragged 5 ft toward the nearest open water and knocked prone.

Cold Lungs (Recharge 5-6). The Given exhales a 15-ft cone of meltwater. DC 12 Constitution save, 10 (3d6) cold damage on a fail, half on a success. On a fail the target's speed is halved until the end of its next turn.

Using them: in Act 2, two Given surfacing near a lingering party is a Hard fight for three (600 adjusted XP) and a solid Medium for four, a warning and not a boss; add a third Given to make it bite at a four-person table. In the climax, do not drop the whole group on the board at once; that is Deadly and swingy. Stagger them, one rising per failed rite round, capped at three Given on the board for a party of three and four for a party of four, so combat pressure tracks the skill challenge and never buries the party under action economy.

Ondre Vael, the Keeper (CR 2, 450 XP, only if it comes to violence)

It should almost never come to this. If cornered and desperate to stop the party exposing her, she defends the rite.

Ondre Vael

AC 13 | HP 45 (7d8+14) | Speed 30 ft

STR 10 (+0) DEX 12 (+1) CON 14 (+2) INT 11 (+0) WIS 16 (+3) CHA 13 (+1)

Saves WIS +5 | Skills Insight +5, Religion +2 | Senses passive Perception 13

Spellcasting. WIS, save DC 13. Cantrips: sacred flame (reflavor as a mote of killing frost), guidance, thaumaturgy. 1st (4 slots): cure wounds, bless, command. 2nd (3 slots): hold person, spiritual weapon (a hook of black ice). 3rd (2 slots): spirit guardians (the cold of the deep).

Actions

Ice hook. Melee, +3 to hit, 4 (1d6+1) piercing plus 2 (1d4) cold.

Roleplay note. Ondre does not want to fight and will stop the instant a player gives her a way out that saves the fjord. A confession beats a battle every time; steer for it.

Hazard: Black Ice (the Glass)

Walking the lake surface. A creature that runs, fights recklessly, or lingers over a groaning patch risks the ice. DC 12 Dexterity (Acrobatics) or Strength (Athletics) to stay up on hard maneuvers; on a fail, prone, and on a natural 1 or a second fail the ice gives way (see Killing Cold). Combat on the Glass imposes disadvantage on the first attack each round unless the creature moves at half speed.

Hazard: the deep's hands

Where the ice is cracked or at the Mouth, She Beneath the Glass reaches. As a lair action on initiative 20, or when the story wants teeth, a hand of black water erupts under one creature within 10 ft of open water: DC 13 Strength save or be grappled (escape DC 13) and pulled 5 ft toward the water. A creature that starts its turn grappled and adjacent to open water takes 5 (2d4) cold damage. This is the deep's pressure; use it to make the Mouth feel alive, not to grind HP.

Hazard: Killing Cold (going in the water)

A creature that falls through the ice or is pulled under is immersed in near-freezing water. At the end of each round submerged or soaked and exposed, it must make a DC 12 Constitution save or gain one level of exhaustion; a soaked creature that cannot get warm within a few minutes keeps saving. Getting a soaked ally out and warm (an action, and a source of heat) stops the clock. This is the quiet threat that makes every hazard above matter; a character does not need to be bitten to be in real danger tonight.

---

The pregens

Four Lake People of the Luneglass Fjord, level 3; run all four, or drop any one for a three-person table. Each has a tie that makes the climax cut, and a survivability lever so a bad round does not wipe a small party. Names use the fjord's cold-water aesthetic, deliberately unlike Elarosean flower-names. Hand these out as-is or let players reskin the flavor.

Néve Underlake, the Given's sister (she/her)

Fighter 3 (Battle Master), Soldier of the ice-shore. The person on the ice tonight is your little sibling, Wren.

Néve, Fighter 3

AC 18 (chain mail, shield) | HP 28 | Speed 30 ft | Prof +2

STR 16 (+3) DEX 12 (+1) CON 14 (+2) INT 10 (+0) WIS 12 (+1) CHA 8 (-1)

Saves STR +5, CON +4 | Skills Athletics +5, Intimidation +1, Perception +3, Survival +3

Fighting Style: Protection (when a creature you can see attacks a target within 5 ft of you, use your reaction and shield to impose disadvantage).

Attacks

Whalebone harpoon. Melee/thrown (20/60), +5, 1d6+3 piercing (1d8 two-handed). You carry three.

Hand-axe. Melee/thrown, +5, 1d6+3.

Features

Second Wind (bonus action, regain 1d10+3 HP, once per rest). Action Surge (one extra action, once per rest). Battle Master: 3 superiority dice (d8), maneuvers: Trip Attack, Riposte, Rally (bonus action, an ally who can hear you gains 1d8+1 temp HP). Know a foe's stats after a fight (Know Your Enemy).

Tie: Wren is everything. Néve does not believe any honor is worth their sister, and has not said so because Wren asked her not to.

Lever: Second Wind plus Rally keeps Néve and one ally standing through the worst round.

Spotlight: the Feast beat in Act 1; protecting a giver at the Mouth.

Corvann Still, the Keeper's apprentice (he/him)

Cleric 3 (Tempest Domain), acolyte of the deep. Ondre Vael raised you in the rite. Tonight you learn what she did.

Corvann, Cleric 3

AC 18 (chain mail, shield) | HP 24 | Speed 30 ft | Prof +2

STR 10 (+0) DEX 12 (+1) CON 14 (+2) INT 10 (+0) WIS 16 (+3) CHA 12 (+1)

Saves WIS +5, CHA +3 | Skills Insight +5, Religion +2, Medicine +5, Persuasion +3

Spellcasting WIS, save DC 13, +5 to hit.

Cantrips: sacred flame (a mote of killing frost), guidance, thaumaturgy, resistance.

1st (4 slots): cure wounds, healing word, bless, plus domain fog cloud and thunderwave.

2nd (3 slots): spiritual weapon (a hook of black ice), lesser restoration, hold person, plus domain gust of wind and shatter.

Features

Channel Divinity (1/rest): Destructive Wrath (maximize thunder or lightning damage) or Turn Undead (the drowned Given are undead). Wrath of the Storm (reaction, when hit in melee, 2d8 thunder/lightning, uses per rest equal to WIS mod).

Tie: faith in the deep, and in Ondre, both about to break. Corvann has spoken the rite as beauty his whole life; tonight he has to decide whether it was ever true.

Lever: Healing Word is a bonus-action ranged heal that keeps a small party alive; Turn Undead scatters the Given at the Mouth.

Spotlight: the Given's-table beat in Act 1; the ledger and the rite's liturgy in Acts 2 and 3.

Iskra Meltwater, the ice-singer (they/them)

Warlock 3 (The Fathomless), raised half outside the fjord in the Lake People's quiet trade of watchers and listeners. Your patron is She Beneath the Glass. You have heard her your whole life.

Iskra, Warlock 3

AC 15 (mage armor, Dex) | HP 20 | Speed 30 ft, swim 30 ft | Prof +2

STR 8 (-1) DEX 14 (+2) CON 14 (+2) INT 12 (+1) WIS 10 (+0) CHA 16 (+3)

Saves WIS +2, CHA +5 | Skills Arcana +3, Deception +5, Insight +2, Investigation +3, Persuasion +5

Spellcasting CHA, save DC 13, +5 to hit. 2 slots, 2nd level, regained on a short rest.

Cantrips: eldritch blast (a lash of black water, two beams, +5, 1d10+3 force each with Agonizing Blast), chill touch, prestidigitation, mage hand.

Spells known: hex, armor of Agathys, misty step, hold person, plus at-will (via patron) an ever-known detect/false step of the deep.

Invocations: Agonizing Blast, Devil's Sight (see 120 ft in magical darkness), Beast Speech.

Features

Tentacle of the Deeps (bonus action, summon a spectral tentacle, 10-ft reach, 1d8 cold and target's speed -10 ft, move it 30 ft each turn). Gift of the Sea (swim speed, breathe water). Pact Magic recovers on a short rest.

Tie: the antagonist is Iskra's patron. She whispers all night. In the cheat ending they are the only one who can speak her tongue; in the break ending they must turn on the power that made them; in either pay ending they know, before anyone tells them, exactly what she wants.

Lever: Armor of Agathys is temp HP plus cold retaliation, and a swim speed plus water-breathing means Iskra is the one party member the Killing Cold does not simply drown.

Spotlight: the old-songs beat in Act 1; reading the deep throughout; the wedge of the climax.

Sable Vesk, the skeptic (he/him)

Ranger 3 (Gloom Stalker), hunter of the frozen coast. Grandchild of Old Sesk, and you have never once believed the Stilling is anything but a drowning the fjord agreed to call a gift.

Sable, Ranger 3

AC 15 (studded leather, Dex) | HP 28 | Speed 30 ft (35 on your first turn of combat) | Prof +2

STR 10 (+0) DEX 16 (+3) CON 14 (+2) INT 10 (+0) WIS 14 (+2) CHA 8 (-1)

Saves STR +2, DEX +5 | Skills Perception +4, Stealth +5, Survival +4, Nature +2, Athletics +2

Fighting Style: Archery (the +2 is already in the attack below). Senses darkvision 60 ft, passive Perception 14.

Attacks

Longbow. Ranged, +7 to hit, range 150/600, 1d8+3 piercing. You carry a full quiver.

Twin shortswords. Melee, +5 to hit, 1d6+3 piercing; a bonus-action off-hand swing hits for 1d6 (no ability mod).

Hunter's Mark. Bonus action, mark one target: +1d6 damage on every hit against it, and advantage to track it. Concentration.

Features

Dread Ambusher. On your first turn of combat you move +5 ft, and if you take the Attack action you make one extra weapon attack that turn dealing +1d8 damage. A brutal opener; save it for a rising Given.

Umbral Sight. In darkness you are invisible to any creature that relies on darkvision to see you. Under the ice, in the Long Dark, this makes you a ghost.

Favored quarry: the drowned. Advantage on Survival to track undead and on Intelligence checks to recall lore about them; the deep's dead hold no mystery for you.

Spellcasting WIS, save DC 12. 3 first-level slots. Known: Hunter's Mark, Cure Wounds (a backup heal for a small party), Ensnaring Strike (meltwater and ice-grass snare a foe, restrained on a failed STR save). Gloom Stalkers also always know Disguise Self.

Tie: grandchild of Old Sesk, the elder who calls the rite a murder. The Given who surfaces under the Longhall floor in Act 1 is your great-uncle Halber, given before you were born. You want the pact broken, and you are the break ending's heart at the table.

Lever: Dread Ambusher front-loads your damage so a striker with 28 HP still hits hard before the ice can answer; Umbral Sight lets you vanish or reposition in the dark; Cure Wounds backs up Corvann so the party is not one healer away from collapse.

Spotlight: the hunter's-gift beat in Act 1; tracking the deep's movement and hunting the Given in Act 2; the loudest voice for breaking the oath at the Mouth.

---

Handout: the Keeper's page

Give this to the players when they find the ledger in the Undercroft. It is the one page Ondre Vael should have burned. Read it in her voice, or hand it over as text.

From the private ledger of the Keeper, thirty winters past

The lot fell on the child. I have written that plainly so that whoever finds this cannot pretend I did not know.

She was small and she was named for the reading of the ice, and she did not understand what the marks on her face meant, and I could not do it. I tell myself I could not do it because it was wrong. That is a lie I have kept warm for a long time. I could not do it because I loved her, and I have never learned to love the whole fjord more than I love one face in it.

So I gave the deep a wayfarer who was dying anyway. I painted the marks on a stranger in the dark and I spoke the rite over the wrong soul, and I walked him out, and the water took him and was quiet, and I believed I had bought a life with a life and cheated no one.

I know now that the deep does not count the way we count. A given soul is given. A traded one is stolen. It has been patient the way the ice is patient. Whoever reads this: the debt is not the wayfarer's, and it is not this year's lot. The debt is her, the child who lived, and the deep has never once forgotten her name. I could not pay it. Perhaps you are braver than I was. Perhaps you are only younger. I am sorry to leave it in your hands. I have been afraid of the ice for thirty years.

---

Appendices

Omen table (d8)

Roll or pick when you want a beat of wrongness between scenes. Each is atmosphere, not a threat by itself.

| d8 | Omen | | --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | 1 | The ice sings, one high pure note, and holds it too long. | | 2 | A dog on the shore will not stop staring at the Mouth and will not be called in. | | 3 | A child says the water spoke to them and repeats a name no living person here carries. | | 4 | Every lamp on the ice gutters blue at once, then steadies. | | 5 | Under the Longhall floor, a second face has surfaced beside the first. | | 6 | Maren goes quiet mid-sentence, listening to the lake, and looks frightened for the first time all night. | | 7 | The new ice at the Mouth has closed another hand's width. Advance the Sealing. | | 8 | Someone finds Given's marks, fresh and blue, painted on their own door in the night. No one admits to it. |

Lake People names (for reskins or extra NPCs)

Cold, water, moon, glass, stillness. Néve, Iskra, Corvann, Maren, Ondre, Sesk, Halber, Tolv, Bec, Wren, Vael, Underlake, Meltwater, Still, Halda, Isk, Marrow, Sable, Lune, Vesk, Thero, Corra, Dunmere, Haldis.

Runtime and pacing

  • Act 1 (~60 to 90 min): do not rush the wake. Its warmth is the whole engine. End on the first crack the moment the room peaks.
  • Act 2 (~60 to 90 min): keep it moving with the Sealing and the omen table. The party does not need every thread; two of the three big reveals is enough to reach a meaningful choice. Steer toward the Ondre confrontation.
  • Act 3 (~45 to 75 min): let the choice breathe, then run the rite quick and loud. Cap the climax at a handful of swinging rounds; when it resolves, cut to the aftermath and end on a face, not an explanation.

On table size (three or four)

  • The four pregens are Néve, Corvann, Iskra, and Sable. Run all four, or drop any one for a three-person table; the story holds either way, since each ties to a different ending and losing one only softens that branch's advocate at the table.
  • The XP math and drowned Given notes above give both budgets. For four, the Act 2 skirmish wants a third Given to stay tense, and the climax can stagger up to a fourth.
  • Protect the healer's action economy at any size: never open a fight with the whole enemy group acting before the party. Stagger.
  • The bonds from Act 1 are load-bearing for a small party. If the wake went cold, hand the players one free reroll at the Mouth anyway; a party this size at level 3 needs a little grace.
  • If you run five, add one Given per extra character in the climax and raise the Cold Lungs save DC by 1.

The real prize

There is a scoreboard tonight, the Warmth of the Wake and the rite's successes, and it is real, so play it straight. But the thing this session is actually for is the moment a player reaches Wren and the serenity cracks, or the moment Maren decides for herself, or the moment Néve will not let go. Award your attention, and the story's weight, to those, not to the dice. Do not tell the players that up front. Let them find out at the end that the night was never about winning.

---

Player handouts (hand these out)

Short character briefs to give each player at the start. They explain the name and the background and stop there; nothing here spoils a GM secret. Print them, or read them aloud, one to each player.

The Sibling

For the player of Néve Underlake (she/her)

Your name is Néve. It is the word for the old snow, the grained ice that packs down hard and does not melt with the first warm day, the snow you can build on. Your family is Underlake, one of the shore-lines that has read the near ice and set the winter nets for longer than anyone here keeps count.

You have a younger sibling, Wren. You taught them to skate before they could properly walk. Your whole life you have stood a half-step in front of them, between Wren and the cold.

A month ago the lot fell, and it fell on Wren. Tomorrow the fjord walks them out onto the Glass and gives them to the water, and everyone you love calls this an honor. Wren has made their peace. Wren asked you not to make a scene.

You have not agreed to anything. You are simply not going to lose them. You have not worked out yet what that costs, or who you would have to become to mean it. Tonight you find out.

The Apprentice

For the player of Corvann Still (he/him)

Your name is Corvann. Your family is Still, the name the Keeper's line has carried since before the rite had a Keeper, and it means what it says: the deep water that does not move, the calm under the ice, the quiet a prayer is supposed to sound like.

Ondre Vael raised you to it. She taught you the songs, the blue marks, the words you speak over the black water so the current stays warm and the fjord does not starve. You have believed your whole life that the Stilling is the oldest and truest love your people have, one life given so that two hundred are kept. You still believe it. Mostly.

Ondre has not slept in a month. She weeps when she thinks you cannot see, and when you ask, she says it is only the cold in her old bones.

Tonight you speak your first rite as a grown priest. You would like, very much, to speak it without your hands shaking.

The Outsider

For the player of Iskra Meltwater (they/them)

Your name is Iskra. It means the spark, the glint of lamplight caught on black ice, the small bright thing the cold did not manage to put out. Your family name is Meltwater, the water that is neither the lake nor the ice but the running water in between, and that has always felt about right.

You were born to the fjord and given young to the watchers, the quiet ones your people send out past the ice to listen to the wider world and carry it home. You have spent years away. You saw a great deal. And the whole time, no matter how far you went, you heard her.

When you were small you went through the ice and did not drown. Something in the deep held you, breathed for you, let you go, and left a mark on you that has never faded. She has whispered to you ever since. She calls you her listener. This winter she has grown louder, and hungrier, and she is the reason you came home.

You do not yet know whether you came back to save this place, or to help her take what she is owed.

The Skeptic

For the player of Sable Vesk (he/him)

Your name is Sable, for the black water under the black ice, the dark you can look down into but never all the way to the bottom. Your family is Vesk, hunters of the frozen coast, and your grandfather is Old Sesk, who says the thing nobody else will say out loud.

He taught you to hunt seal at the breathing-holes and to read a hungry lake, and he taught you the other thing too: that the Stilling is not love, whatever the songs say. It is a drowning the fjord has agreed to call a gift. Forty years ago they gave your grandfather's brother to the water. You never met him. You have spent your whole life being told to be grateful for a bargain paid in your own blood.

Tomorrow they mean to walk another one out onto the ice. You came to the wake anyway, because Wren deserves at least one face at the fire that is not lying to them.

And because you have decided, somewhere you have not said out loud yet, that this is the last one. One way or another.

Elarosea

Developing A floral-themed queendom of four major city-states, ruled by [[Queen Acantha Rosaceae]]

Elarosea

Quick Reference

Parent Region: Caegin

Climate:

Terrain:

Map Image:

Overview

Geography

The Imperial Shelf forms the natural southern border of the queendom. Below it to the southwest lie the Apporiclave Peaks.

Settlements

  • Loquat — center of knowledge and academia
  • Blackthorn — center of combat and military might, known for grand forges
  • Cinquefoil — center of culture and the arts
  • Medlar — center of commerce and economy

Powers & Politics

  • 'The Crown' is the common shorthand for the Queen herself and, loosely, any arm of her government.
  • Elarosea maintains a standing military.
  • Two private forces answer directly to Queen Acantha Rosaceae and to no one else: the Queen's Thorns (enforcement, brutal efficiency) and the Queen's Roots (intelligence, no scruple against blackmail or coercion).

Connections

  • Caegin
  • Queen Acantha Rosaceae
  • Loquat
  • Blackthorn
  • Cinquefoil
  • Medlar

Luneglass Fjord

Quick Reference

Climate: unknown

Terrain: fjord

Map Image:

Overview

The Luneglass Fjord lies outside Elarosea's borders and is home to a people known as the Lake People. They are not a known political presence within Elarosea, but they appear to maintain at least some intelligence capacity: Alicia, mother of Dez, was dispatched from among them as a spy into Elarosean territory. What drives any tension or interest between the Lake People and Elarosea is not yet known.

Edmund Vane's True Origins

Developing Edmund Vane entered the Hive as a planted Queen's Roots spy and was later turned by Elara after she discovered him Known to: Elara Vane, Edmund Vane

Edmund Vane's True Origins

Quick Reference

Status: secret

Truth: true

Known By: Elara Vane, Edmund Vane

About: Edmund Vane, The Hive, Queen's Roots

Source: Hive internal history

The Rumor

Edmund Vane did not join the Hive as a genuine recruit. He was planted there by the Queen's Roots to gather intelligence on Elara Vane's operation and report back to the Crown. The Roots assigned Edmund to infiltrate the Hive and spy on Elara's network. During his time undercover, Elara saved his life, creating a genuine bond between them. When she eventually uncovered his role she chose to spare him rather than treat him as a traitor. He left the Roots and now serves as her second in command. The shared surname with Elara is coincidence, not family.

The Truth

Find the Others

Developing The party's shared vision at Benz: 'find the others before she does'; 'she' is Acantha, the others are the unconsumed gods Aeolus, Valerius, and Alithiel Known to: Vesperae Locke, Dez, Kirill Amaterasu, Layla, Calanthe Orchidaceae, Cleke

Find the Others

Quick Reference

Status: partially revealed

Truth: true

Known By: Vesperae Locke, Dez, Kirill Amaterasu, Layla, Calanthe Orchidaceae, Cleke

About: God of Second Chances, Queen Acantha Rosaceae, Aeolus, Valerius, Alithiel

Source: Shared divine vision at Benz after fragment activation, 7 Pollant 301

The Rumor

After their God of Second Chances fragments activated in Benz, every party member simultaneously received the same vision: "Find the others... before she does." The source of the vision, the identity of "the others," and the identity of "she" are all unknown.

  • 24 Pollant 301: Valerius located and confirmed imprisoned at Crown Works Installation Seven; see Valerius Found Imprisoned at Crown Works. Aeolus and Alithiel remain unfound.
  • 24 Pollant 301: Valerius located, caged over the central chasm of Crown Works Installation Seven; identity confirmed by Nal'Szomn. Aeolus and Alithiel remain unfound.
  • 24 Pollant 301: Valerius freed by the party during the escape from Crown Works Installation Seven: the first of the three reached, now loose in Blackthorn and unclaimed. See Valerius Freed at Crown Works. Aeolus and Alithiel remain unfound. (Table correction, recorded 2026-07-12.)

The Truth

"She" is Queen Acantha Rosaceae. The others are the three gods not yet consumed by the Queen: Aeolus, Valerius, and Alithiel. The vision warns the party to reach these gods before Acantha does. Valerius was found but not by Queen Acantha Rosaceae: Sable used The Aether Golem to consume him on 25 Pollant 301. The remaining unconsumed gods Aeolus and Alithiel are still at large. See Aether Golem Consumes Valerius.

Leads

Connections

  • God of Second Chances
  • Benz
  • Session 1

Party Blamed for the Benz Incident

Developing The Crown publicly frames the party for the destruction of Benz, charging them with treason and terrorism

Party Blamed for the Benz Incident

Quick Reference

Status: partially revealed

Truth: false

Known By:

About: Benz

Source: Crown official proclamation following the Benz incident

The Rumor

The Crown's official account: the village of Benz was destroyed in Year 301 by a terrorist attack carried out by the party. The party is charged with Treason, Sedition, Arson, and other crimes and is wanted throughout Elarosea.

The Truth: A fallen star released alien creatures that killed the villagers. The party defeated the creatures. The Queen's Thorns then killed every other witness to suppress the event. The party is framed specifically because they are the only surviving loose ends.

Leads: The party themselves; any surviving trace of the alien creatures or the crashed star; anyone willing to investigate the Thorns' movements that night. The charges against the party include treason, sedition, and arson. The rest of the list is not tracked: the full list of charges does not matter in play. The frame covers the Massacre at Benz at Benz, which a The Thorns commander ordered.

The Truth

Theron's True Death

Developing Theron Blackthorn did not die with honour; Roots agents killed him on Crown orders and his father signed the warrant. Sable confirmed under Zone of Truth that she traces his death to the Queen and named Corvus's inaction as part of the cause. Known to: Sable, Elara Vane, Edmund Vane, Cassimir Blackthorn, Val, Calanthe Orchidaceae, Dez, Kirill Amaterasu, Vesperae Locke, Spadae

Theron's True Death

Quick Reference

Status: partially revealed

Truth: partial

Known By: Sable, Elara Vane, Edmund Vane, Cassimir Blackthorn

About: Theron Blackthorn

Source: Hive intelligence

The Rumor

On 24 Pollant 301, Maret Voss told Cassimir Blackthorn directly that the Blackthorn Writings are truly his brother's, that Lord Corvus Blackthorn signed the death warrant, and that by harnessing the god in Blackthorn she can bring Theron back. See Theft of the Aether Core. (Corrected 2026-07-12: an earlier digest recorded her claiming Theron was alive and merely captured; that was wrong. She never claimed he lives. Death, not capture, is the reality, and her bring-him-back promise is false.)

  • 24 Pollant 301 Bloom: Sable confirmed under Zone of Truth that Queen Acantha Rosaceae killed Theron Blackthorn and that Lord Corvus Blackthorn's inaction enabled it. She also confirmed she cannot raise him: the God of Death was consumed by the Queen. See Sable Questioned Under Zone of Truth.
  • 24 Pollant 301 Bloom: Nal'Szomn confirmed in The Dreamcatcher that only the God of Death can touch the dead, and Queen Acantha Rosaceae has consumed him. Sable cannot keep her promise to Cassimir Blackthorn regardless of what god she cages. The party knows; Cassimir does not. See Party Visits the Dreamcatcher.
  • 24 Pollant 301 Bloom: Under Zone of Truth at Blackthorn Training Grounds, Sable confirmed she traces Theron Blackthorn's death to Queen Acantha Rosaceae and named Lord Corvus Blackthorn's inaction as part of the cause. The core accusation is now partially confirmed; the Roots warrant and Corvus's direct signing of it remain unverified in-play. See Sable Questioned Under Zone of Truth.

The Truth

The public account is that Theron Blackthorn died with honour in his final military campaign. Elara Vane's Hive and Sable both know he was backstabbed by agents of Queen Acantha Rosaceae's Roots organization. Until 24 Pollant 301 nobody outside the Hive held this knowledge, and Sable withheld it from Cassimir Blackthorn while using her Maret Voss cover to earn his trust, keeping him emotionally dependent on the grief she pretends to share.

Theron is gone. As of 24 Pollant 301, Sable has told Cassimir a version of the betrayal (the Crown's hand, the warrant his father signed) and paired it with a promise she knows is false: that by harnessing the god in Blackthorn she can bring Theron back. The resurrection is the leash. Who or what actually authors the Blackthorn Writings remains unknown.

Leads

Connections

  • Theron Blackthorn
  • Elara Vane
  • Sable
  • Cassimir Blackthorn
  • Queen Acantha Rosaceae
  • Maret Voss
  • Session 12
  • Edmund Vane The Roots
  • Edmund Names Sable

Log

  • 24 Pollant 301 Bloom: Edmund Vane tells the party directly that Theron died at The Roots' hands and that Sable's resurrection promise is a lie. See Edmund Names Sable.

Edmund Vane confirmed on 24 Pollant 301 that Theron was taken off the field by The Roots and confirmed dead within the week. The party now knows the death is real and Sable's resurrection promise is false. The Crown's role in ordering the death and Lord Corvus Blackthorn's signature on the warrant remain unconfirmed from the party's perspective.

Description

Edmund Vane confirmed the killing under Zone of Truth on 25 Pollant 301; the agents who carried it out belonged to The Roots, the Crown's covert intelligence arm.
